[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is… at night, after dinner, I’ll start picking at leftovers in the kitchen, and I tell myself, “That’s enough, Alejandro,” but somehow my hand just keeps going back. It’s like the sensible part of me is there, talking, but it’s not really in charge.  \n\nAnd, you know, I never used to think much of leftovers—my mother hated wasting food, so that’s probably stuck with me—but this feels different. I’ll mean to stop, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset when you start picking at leftovers?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. Sometimes I think I’m just… wandering into the kitchen because it’s there. I wouldn’t always call it stress or anxiety exactly.\n\nThough evenings can be odd, you know—too quiet sometimes. After dinner the house settles down, and I’ll notice the dishes or the little container of rice sitting there, and then I’m thinking about my mother telling us not to waste food. She used to wrap everything up so carefully. But whether I’m upset when it starts… I don’t know that I’ve paid close attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sexually aroused when you're picking at leftovers?\n\nPatient: Sexually aroused? No, I don’t think so. I mean, that’s not something I’ve connected to it at all. It’s more like… I’m standing there by the counter and one bite turns into another.\n\nFunny, the kitchen light at night reminds me of my parents’ old house—my mother used to leave this little yellow bulb on over the stove. But no, to your question, I haven’t noticed anything sexual about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat the leftovers, like something bad would happen if you didn't?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it feels like something bad would happen, exactly. Not like… if I leave the container there, the house is going to fall apart or anything.\n\nIt’s more that I see it, and then I start thinking, well, it shouldn’t go to waste. My mother was very strong about that—she could make a soup out of almost anything, honestly. But whether I feel I have to eat it because I’m afraid of some consequence… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty if you don't finish the leftovers?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean, there’s a little voice about waste, I suppose, but that’s been there since I was a kid. My mother could stretch leftovers for days—she had this way of making it seem almost wrong to throw anything out.\n\nBut if I don’t finish them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed whether I feel guilty after. Sometimes I just put the lid back on and tell myself I’ll deal with it tomorrow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I tell myself I’m just having a bite, just tidying up the container a little, but then… once I start picking at it, it’s like the sensible part of me is saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, close the fridge.\n\nAnd my hand still goes back in anyway. It reminds me of when my uncle used to stand in front of the icebox after dinner—he’d say he was “checking the inventory,” which was ridiculous. But yes, with me, late at night especially, I don’t feel quite in control once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you feel better when you're feeling down or bored in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. In the evenings, I do get sort of restless, and the kitchen is just… there. But whether it makes me feel better, I don’t know if I’ve paid close enough attention.\n\nSometimes it’s more like something to do with my hands, you know? My father used to sit with a newspaper for hours after dinner, same chair every night, and I never understood how he could just sit still like that. I tend to wander. I’ll put a cup in the sink, notice the container, open it…\n\nBut feeling better? Maybe for a minute, or maybe I’m just distracted. I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to eat the leftovers when you see them?\n\nPatient: When I just see them? Not always overwhelming right away, no. I can open the fridge and notice them and think, oh, there’s the chicken or the rice or whatever, and sometimes I close it.\n\nBut late at night, if I start picking at them, that’s where it changes. It’s like part of me is saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away, and then my hand just keeps going back. My aunt used to say food “calls to you” from the icebox, which I always thought was silly, but… sometimes it does feel a little like that once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating the leftovers because you feel like you have to, or is it more because you want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it feels like a clear “want,” exactly. I mean, I do want it in the moment, I suppose, or I wouldn’t open the container. But what brings me in is more that once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it stops feeling like a choice.\n\nIt’s like the sensible part of me is saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, close it up,” and then my hand keeps going back for more anyway. My mother used to save every scrap in little bowls with foil over them, and you’d have to sort of excavate the fridge to find anything… but with me, it’s not really about enjoying the leftovers. It’s that once I’ve started, I have a hard time stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you deal with any uncomfortable feelings or emotions you might be having in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it helps me deal with feelings, exactly. I don’t always know what I’m feeling in the evening, to be honest. Sometimes it’s just quiet in the house, and then I notice the fridge, and then one thing leads to another.\n\nI had a neighbor years ago who used to walk every night after dinner, rain or shine, and I used to think, well, that’s a good habit, isn’t it? Me, I tend to drift around the kitchen instead. But whether I’m trying to push down sadness or boredom or anything like that… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It may be more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to calm down or relax when you're feeling restless in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, like calming down or relaxing. Maybe there’s some of that, but I don’t really notice myself thinking, “I need to settle down, let me eat.” It’s more like I’m in the kitchen, a little aimless, and then I see the container.\n\nRestless, yes, evenings can feel sort of… loose, you know? After dinner there isn’t always much structure. When I was working, at least you had the next morning sort of pressing on you. Now the hours stretch out. My brother-in-law used to do crossword puzzles every night with a cup of tea, very orderly man. I never got into that.\n\nSo I don’t know if the leftovers relax me. They might just give me something to do for a few minutes, and then it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you weren't able to eat the leftovers when you want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant distress. I mean, if the leftovers weren’t there, I suppose I might feel annoyed or sort of unsettled for a bit, but I’m not sure. Sometimes I don’t even know I want them until I’m already standing there with the fridge open.\n\nIt’s funny, my aunt had this old refrigerator that made such a racket you could hear it from the living room, and every time it kicked on everyone would joke the house was about to take off. Mine is quieter, so maybe I notice the light more than the sound—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really tested it, I guess. I don’t know that I’d be deeply upset if I couldn’t eat them. I might just grumble and look around for something else to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to eat the leftovers, even if you're not hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think compelled might be the word, though I don’t love admitting that. It’s not always that I’m hungry. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, there’s this part of me going, “That’s enough, Alejandro, leave it alone,” but my hand still goes back in the container.\n\nIt reminds me of when my mother used to say you had to put food away properly or everyone would keep “evening it out,” taking little bites until it was gone. I used to laugh at that, but now I sort of do it myself. I’ll tell myself I’m just having one more forkful, and then somehow I’m back again. So yes, there is a feeling of not quite steering it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers even if you weren't thinking about your mother or your childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that my mother or childhood is always in my head when it happens. Sometimes those memories come up after I’ve already noticed what I’m doing, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing there with the leftovers, and then I think of something she used to say, but I don’t know if that’s the reason I’m eating them. It might just be… the food is there. The kitchen is quiet. I’m up later than I meant to be.\n\nMy mother had all these little sayings about food, but then again everyone’s mother did, probably. So I don’t want to make too much of it. I guess I haven’t really separated it out enough to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you avoid feeling empty or bored when you're alone in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Bored maybe, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’m eating the leftovers to avoid that feeling.\n\nI’ll wander into the kitchen and then I’m there, you know. I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to eat the leftovers when you see them?\n\nPatient: When I just see them? Not always overwhelming right away, no. I can open the fridge and notice them and think, oh, there’s the chicken or the rice or whatever, and sometimes I close it.\n\nBut late at night, if I start picking at them, that’s where it changes. It’s like part of me is saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away, and then my hand just keeps going back. My aunt used to say food “calls to you” from the icebox, which I always thought was silly, but… sometimes it does feel a little like that once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating the leftovers because you feel like you have to, or is it more because you want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it feels like a clear “want,” exactly. I mean, I do want it in the moment, I suppose, or I wouldn’t open the container. But what brings me in is more that once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it stops feeling like a choice.\n\nIt’s like the sensible part of me is saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, close it up,” and then my hand keeps going back for more anyway. My mother used to save every scrap in little bowls with foil over them, and you’d have to sort of excavate the fridge to find anything… but with me, it’s not really about enjoying the leftovers. It’s that once I’ve started, I have a hard time stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you deal with any uncomfortable feelings or emotions you might be having in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it helps me deal with feelings, exactly. I don’t always know what I’m feeling in the evening, to be honest. Sometimes it’s just quiet in the house, and then I notice the fridge, and then one thing leads to another.\n\nI had a neighbor years ago who used to walk every night after dinner, rain or shine, and I used to think, well, that’s a good habit, isn’t it? Me, I tend to drift around the kitchen instead. But whether I’m trying to push down sadness or boredom or anything like that… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It may be more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to calm down or relax when you're feeling restless in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, like calming down or relaxing. Maybe there’s some of that, but I don’t really notice myself thinking, “I need to settle down, let me eat.” It’s more like I’m in the kitchen, a little aimless, and then I see the container.\n\nRestless, yes, evenings can feel sort of… loose, you know? After dinner there isn’t always much structure. When I was working, at least you had the next morning sort of pressing on you. Now the hours stretch out. My brother-in-law used to do crossword puzzles every night with a cup of tea, very orderly man. I never got into that.\n\nSo I don’t know if the leftovers relax me. They might just give me something to do for a few minutes, and then it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you weren't able to eat the leftovers when you want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant distress. I mean, if the leftovers weren’t there, I suppose I might feel annoyed or sort of unsettled for a bit, but I’m not sure. Sometimes I don’t even know I want them until I’m already standing there with the fridge open.\n\nIt’s funny, my aunt had this old refrigerator that made such a racket you could hear it from the living room, and every time it kicked on everyone would joke the house was about to take off. Mine is quieter, so maybe I notice the light more than the sound—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really tested it, I guess. I don’t know that I’d be deeply upset if I couldn’t eat them. I might just grumble and look around for something else to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to eat the leftovers, even if you're not hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think compelled might be the word, though I don’t love admitting that. It’s not always that I’m hungry. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, there’s this part of me going, “That’s enough, Alejandro, leave it alone,” but my hand still goes back in the container.\n\nIt reminds me of when my mother used to say you had to put food away properly or everyone would keep “evening it out,” taking little bites until it was gone. I used to laugh at that, but now I sort of do it myself. I’ll tell myself I’m just having one more forkful, and then somehow I’m back again. So yes, there is a feeling of not quite steering it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers even if you weren't thinking about your mother or your childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that my mother or childhood is always in my head when it happens. Sometimes those memories come up after I’ve already noticed what I’m doing, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing there with the leftovers, and then I think of something she used to say, but I don’t know if that’s the reason I’m eating them. It might just be… the food is there. The kitchen is quiet. I’m up later than I meant to be.\n\nMy mother had all these little sayings about food, but then again everyone’s mother did, probably. So I don’t want to make too much of it. I guess I haven’t really separated it out enough to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you avoid feeling empty or bored when you're alone in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, evenings can be quiet, sure, but I haven’t really thought of it as “empty” exactly. Bored maybe, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’m eating the leftovers to avoid that feeling.\n\nI’ll wander into the kitchen and then I’m there, you know. It’s like when you go into a room and forget why you came in—my neighbor used to say that was just “senior moments,” though he said that about everything, even when he was forty-five. Anyway… I’m not sure I’ve connected it that clearly.\n\nIt might be something to do with being alone in the evening, but I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is more about dealing with how you're feeling at the moment or just about the food itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pull those apart neatly. Sometimes it feels like it’s just the food itself—like it’s sitting there, already cooked, and I know it’ll taste good cold even though my daughter says cold pasta is a crime. She’s very firm about that.\n\nBut then… maybe if I were feeling different, I wouldn’t go back to it as much. I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’ll eat this.” It’s more automatic than that. I notice the container, take a bite, then another.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know if it’s the feeling or the food. Maybe both, but I’d be guessing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is something you do to manage or cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. “Cope with emotions” sounds… more organized than it feels when I’m doing it. I’m not usually thinking, well, I’m sad or tense, so I’ll eat the leftovers.\n\nSometimes I suppose I might feel better for a few minutes, but then again sometimes I’m just standing at the fridge because the container is right there. My brother used to eat straight from the pot, which drove my father crazy—he’d say, “Use a plate, we’re not animals,” and now I hear that sometimes when I’m leaning over the sink.\n\nSo… maybe it’s connected to feelings, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it clearly. I wouldn’t want to give you an answer just because it sounds like the right explanation.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure I’d call it regulating, exactly. That sounds like something you do on purpose, like checking the thermostat or taking deep breaths or… I don’t know, those little meditation tapes my wife used to have with the ocean sounds. I always just ended up thinking about whether the tide was coming in or going out.

With the leftovers, I don’t really know what it’s doing emotionally. Sometimes I feel calmer while I’m eating, maybe, but I couldn’t say if that’s the point of it or just because eating is eating. It’s more like I notice the food and then I’m eating it before I’ve really thought through why.

So… maybe in some way, but I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes.

Me, I tend to drift around the kitchen instead. But whether I’m trying to push down sadness or boredom or anything like that… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It may be more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to calm down or relax when you're feeling restless in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, like calming down or relaxing. After dinner there isn’t always much structure. When I was working, at least you had the next morning sort of pressing on you. Now the hours stretch out. My brother-in-law used to do crossword puzzles every night with a cup of tea, very orderly man. I never got into that.\n\nSo I don’t know if the leftovers relax me. They might just give me something to do for a few minutes, and then it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you weren't able to eat the leftovers when you want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant distress. I mean, if the leftovers weren’t there, I suppose I might feel annoyed or sort of unsettled for a bit, but I’m not sure. Sometimes I don’t even know I want them until I’m already standing there with the fridge open.\n\nIt’s funny, my aunt had this old refrigerator that made such a racket you could hear it from the living room, and every time it kicked on everyone would joke the house was about to take off. Mine is quieter, so maybe I notice the light more than the sound—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really tested it, I guess. I don’t know that I’d be deeply upset if I couldn’t eat them. I might just grumble and look around for something else to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to eat the leftovers, even if you're not hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think compelled might be the word, though I don’t love admitting that. It’s not always that I’m hungry. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, there’s this part of me going, “That’s enough, Alejandro, leave it alone,” but my hand still goes back in the container.\n\nIt reminds me of when my mother used to say you had to put food away properly or everyone would keep “evening it out,” taking little bites until it was gone. I used to laugh at that, but now I sort of do it myself. I’ll tell myself I’m just having one more forkful, and then somehow I’m back again. So yes, there is a feeling of not quite steering it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers even if you weren't thinking about your mother or your childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that my mother or childhood is always in my head when it happens. Sometimes those memories come up after I’ve already noticed what I’m doing, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing there with the leftovers, and then I think of something she used to say, but I don’t know if that’s the reason I’m eating them. It might just be… the food is there. The kitchen is quiet. I’m up later than I meant to be.\n\nMy mother had all these little sayings about food, but then again everyone’s mother did, probably. So I don’t want to make too much of it. I guess I haven’t really separated it out enough to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you avoid feeling empty or bored when you're alone in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, evenings can be quiet, sure, but I haven’t really thought of it as “empty” exactly. Bored maybe, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’m eating the leftovers to avoid that feeling.\n\nI’ll wander into the kitchen and then I’m there, you know. It’s like when you go into a room and forget why you came in—my neighbor used to say that was just “senior moments,” though he said that about everything, even when he was forty-five. Anyway… I’m not sure I’ve connected it that clearly.\n\nIt might be something to do with being alone in the evening, but I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is more about dealing with how you're feeling at the moment or just about the food itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pull those apart neatly. Sometimes it feels like it’s just the food itself—like it’s sitting there, already cooked, and I know it’ll taste good cold even though my daughter says cold pasta is a crime. She’s very firm about that.\n\nBut then… maybe if I were feeling different, I wouldn’t go back to it as much. I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’ll eat this.” It’s more automatic than that. I notice the container, take a bite, then another.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know if it’s the feeling or the food. Maybe both, but I’d be guessing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is something you do to manage or cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. “Cope with emotions” sounds… more organized than it feels when I’m doing it. I’m not usually thinking, well, I’m sad or tense, so I’ll eat the leftovers.\n\nSometimes I suppose I might feel better for a few minutes, but then again sometimes I’m just standing at the fridge because the container is right there. My brother used to eat straight from the pot, which drove my father crazy—he’d say, “Use a plate, we’re not animals,” and now I hear that sometimes when I’m leaning over the sink.\n\nSo… maybe it’s connected to feelings, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it clearly. I wouldn’t want to give you an answer just because it sounds like the right explanation.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the act of eating leftovers is something that helps you regulate or manage your emotions in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it regulating, exactly. That sounds like something you do on purpose, like checking the thermostat or taking deep breaths or… I don’t know, those little meditation tapes my wife used to have with the ocean sounds. I always just ended up thinking about whether the tide was coming in or going out.\n\nWith the leftovers, I don’t really know what it’s doing emotionally. Sometimes I feel calmer while I’m eating, maybe, but I couldn’t say if that’s the point of it or just because eating is eating. It’s more like I notice the food and then I’m eating it before I’ve really thought through why.\n\nSo… maybe in some way, but I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s like the sensible part of me is there, talking, but it’s not really in charge.  \n\nAnd, you know, I never used to think much of leftovers—my mother hated wasting food, so that’s probably stuck with me—but this feels different. That sounds like something you do on purpose, like checking the thermostat or taking deep breaths or… I don’t know, those little meditation tapes my wife used to have with the ocean sounds. I always just ended up thinking about whether the tide was coming in or going out.\n\nWith the leftovers, I don’t really know what it’s doing emotionally. Sometimes I feel calmer while I’m eating, maybe, but I couldn’t say if that’s the point of it or just because eating is eating. It’s more like I notice the food and then I’m eating it before I’ve really thought through why.\n\nSo… maybe in some way, but I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still eat the leftovers if you were in a very good mood?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Eating_Without_Hunger\nmanifestation: \"A lot of the time I’m not even hungry, because I already had a good dinner with everyone, but later I still find myself opening containers and tasting one thing after another.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think I probably would. I mean, if I’ve already had dinner with everyone—proper dinner, not just picking at something—I’m usually not hungry later. But I still end up opening the fridge and, you know, lifting the lid on one container, tasting a bit of this, then maybe something else.

It’s funny, my mother used to save everything in those margarine tubs, so you never knew if you were getting butter or gravy. Maybe that’s why I still have to look in all the containers. But yes… even in a good mood, I think I might still do it. It doesn’t always seem to start from feeling bad. Sometimes I’m just not hungry, but I’m eating the leftovers anyway.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is… at night, after dinner, I’ll start picking at leftovers in the kitchen, and I tell myself, “That’s enough, Alejandro,” but somehow my hand just keeps going back. It’s like the sensible part of me is there, talking, but it’s not really in charge.  \n\nAnd, you know, I never used to think much of leftovers—my mother hated wasting food, so that’s probably stuck with me—but this feels different. I’ll mean to stop, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset when you start picking at leftovers?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. Sometimes I think I’m just… wandering into the kitchen because it’s there. I wouldn’t always call it stress or anxiety exactly.\n\nThough evenings can be odd, you know—too quiet sometimes. After dinner the house settles down, and I’ll notice the dishes or the little container of rice sitting there, and then I’m thinking about my mother telling us not to waste food. She used to wrap everything up so carefully. But whether I’m upset when it starts… I don’t know that I’ve paid close attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sexually aroused when you're picking at leftovers?\n\nPatient: Sexually aroused? No, I don’t think so. I mean, that’s not something I’ve connected to it at all. It’s more like… I’m standing there by the counter and one bite turns into another.\n\nFunny, the kitchen light at night reminds me of my parents’ old house—my mother used to leave this little yellow bulb on over the stove. But no, to your question, I haven’t noticed anything sexual about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat the leftovers, like something bad would happen if you didn't?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it feels like something bad would happen, exactly. Not like… if I leave the container there, the house is going to fall apart or anything.\n\nIt’s more that I see it, and then I start thinking, well, it shouldn’t go to waste. My mother was very strong about that—she could make a soup out of almost anything, honestly. But whether I feel I have to eat it because I’m afraid of some consequence… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty if you don't finish the leftovers?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean, there’s a little voice about waste, I suppose, but that’s been there since I was a kid. My mother could stretch leftovers for days—she had this way of making it seem almost wrong to throw anything out.\n\nBut if I don’t finish them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed whether I feel guilty after. Sometimes I just put the lid back on and tell myself I’ll deal with it tomorrow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I tell myself I’m just having a bite, just tidying up the container a little, but then… once I start picking at it, it’s like the sensible part of me is saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, close the fridge.\n\nAnd my hand still goes back in anyway. It reminds me of when my uncle used to stand in front of the icebox after dinner—he’d say he was “checking the inventory,” which was ridiculous. But yes, with me, late at night especially, I don’t feel quite in control once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you feel better when you're feeling down or bored in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. In the evenings, I do get sort of restless, and the kitchen is just… there. But whether it makes me feel better, I don’t know if I’ve paid close enough attention.\n\nSometimes it’s more like something to do with my hands, you know? My father used to sit with a newspaper for hours after dinner, same chair every night, and I never understood how he could just sit still like that. I tend to wander. I’ll put a cup in the sink, notice the container, open it…\n\nBut feeling better? Maybe for a minute, or maybe I’m just distracted. I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to eat the leftovers when you see them?\n\nPatient: When I just see them? Not always overwhelming right away, no. I can open the fridge and notice them and think, oh, there’s the chicken or the rice or whatever, and sometimes I close it.\n\nBut late at night, if I start picking at them, that’s where it changes. It’s like part of me is saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away, and then my hand just keeps going back. My aunt used to say food “calls to you” from the icebox, which I always thought was silly, but… sometimes it does feel a little like that once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating the leftovers because you feel like you have to, or is it more because you want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it feels like a clear “want,” exactly. I mean, I do want it in the moment, I suppose, or I wouldn’t open the container. But what brings me in is more that once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it stops feeling like a choice.\n\nIt’s like the sensible part of me is saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, close it up,” and then my hand keeps going back for more anyway. My mother used to save every scrap in little bowls with foil over them, and you’d have to sort of excavate the fridge to find anything… but with me, it’s not really about enjoying the leftovers. It’s that once I’ve started, I have a hard time stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you deal with any uncomfortable feelings or emotions you might be having in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it helps me deal with feelings, exactly. I don’t always know what I’m feeling in the evening, to be honest. Sometimes it’s just quiet in the house, and then I notice the fridge, and then one thing leads to another.\n\nI had a neighbor years ago who used to walk every night after dinner, rain or shine, and I used to think, well, that’s a good habit, isn’t it? Me, I tend to drift around the kitchen instead. But whether I’m trying to push down sadness or boredom or anything like that… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It may be more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to calm down or relax when you're feeling restless in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, like calming down or relaxing. Maybe there’s some of that, but I don’t really notice myself thinking, “I need to settle down, let me eat.” It’s more like I’m in the kitchen, a little aimless, and then I see the container.\n\nRestless, yes, evenings can feel sort of… loose, you know? After dinner there isn’t always much structure. When I was working, at least you had the next morning sort of pressing on you. Now the hours stretch out. My brother-in-law used to do crossword puzzles every night with a cup of tea, very orderly man. I never got into that.\n\nSo I don’t know if the leftovers relax me. They might just give me something to do for a few minutes, and then it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you weren't able to eat the leftovers when you want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant distress. I mean, if the leftovers weren’t there, I suppose I might feel annoyed or sort of unsettled for a bit, but I’m not sure. Sometimes I don’t even know I want them until I’m already standing there with the fridge open.\n\nIt’s funny, my aunt had this old refrigerator that made such a racket you could hear it from the living room, and every time it kicked on everyone would joke the house was about to take off. Mine is quieter, so maybe I notice the light more than the sound—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really tested it, I guess. I don’t know that I’d be deeply upset if I couldn’t eat them. I might just grumble and look around for something else to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to eat the leftovers, even if you're not hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think compelled might be the word, though I don’t love admitting that. It’s not always that I’m hungry. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, there’s this part of me going, “That’s enough, Alejandro, leave it alone,” but my hand still goes back in the container.\n\nIt reminds me of when my mother used to say you had to put food away properly or everyone would keep “evening it out,” taking little bites until it was gone. I used to laugh at that, but now I sort of do it myself. I’ll tell myself I’m just having one more forkful, and then somehow I’m back again. So yes, there is a feeling of not quite steering it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers even if you weren't thinking about your mother or your childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that my mother or childhood is always in my head when it happens. Sometimes those memories come up after I’ve already noticed what I’m doing, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing there with the leftovers, and then I think of something she used to say, but I don’t know if that’s the reason I’m eating them. It might just be… the food is there. The kitchen is quiet. I’m up later than I meant to be.\n\nMy mother had all these little sayings about food, but then again everyone’s mother did, probably. So I don’t want to make too much of it. I guess I haven’t really separated it out enough to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you avoid feeling empty or bored when you're alone in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, evenings can be quiet, sure, but I haven’t really thought of it as “empty” exactly. Bored maybe, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’m eating the leftovers to avoid that feeling.\n\nI’ll wander into the kitchen and then I’m there, you know. It’s like when you go into a room and forget why you came in—my neighbor used to say that was just “senior moments,” though he said that about everything, even when he was forty-five. Anyway… I’m not sure I’ve connected it that clearly.\n\nIt might be something to do with being alone in the evening, but I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is more about dealing with how you're feeling at the moment or just about the food itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pull those apart neatly. Sometimes it feels like it’s just the food itself—like it’s sitting there, already cooked, and I know it’ll taste good cold even though my daughter says cold pasta is a crime. She’s very firm about that.\n\nBut then… maybe if I were feeling different, I wouldn’t go back to it as much. I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’ll eat this.” It’s more automatic than that. I notice the container, take a bite, then another.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know if it’s the feeling or the food. Maybe both, but I’d be guessing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is something you do to manage or cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. “Cope with emotions” sounds… more organized than it feels when I’m doing it. I’m not usually thinking, well, I’m sad or tense, so I’ll eat the leftovers.\n\nSometimes I suppose I might feel better for a few minutes, but then again sometimes I’m just standing at the fridge because the container is right there. My brother used to eat straight from the pot, which drove my father crazy—he’d say, “Use a plate, we’re not animals,” and now I hear that sometimes when I’m leaning over the sink.\n\nSo… maybe it’s connected to feelings, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it clearly. I wouldn’t want to give you an answer just because it sounds like the right explanation.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the act of eating leftovers is something that helps you regulate or manage your emotions in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it regulating, exactly. That sounds like something you do on purpose, like checking the thermostat or taking deep breaths or… I don’t know, those little meditation tapes my wife used to have with the ocean sounds. I always just ended up thinking about whether the tide was coming in or going out.\n\nWith the leftovers, I don’t really know what it’s doing emotionally. Sometimes I feel calmer while I’m eating, maybe, but I couldn’t say if that’s the point of it or just because eating is eating. It’s more like I notice the food and then I’m eating it before I’ve really thought through why.\n\nSo… maybe in some way, but I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers if you were in a very good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would. I mean, if I’ve already had dinner with everyone—proper dinner, not just picking at something—I’m usually not hungry later. But I still end up opening the fridge and, you know, lifting the lid on one container, tasting a bit of this, then maybe something else.\n\nIt’s funny, my mother used to save everything in those margarine tubs, so you never knew if you were getting butter or gravy. Maybe that’s why I still have to look in all the containers. But yes… even in a good mood, I think I might still do it. It doesn’t always seem to start from feeling bad. Sometimes I’m just not hungry, but I’m eating the leftovers anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.8", "F52.7"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Anyway… I’m not sure I’ve connected it that clearly.\n\nIt might be something to do with being alone in the evening, but I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is more about dealing with how you're feeling at the moment or just about the food itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pull those apart neatly. Sometimes it feels like it’s just the food itself—like it’s sitting there, already cooked, and I know it’ll taste good cold even though my daughter says cold pasta is a crime. She’s very firm about that.\n\nBut then… maybe if I were feeling different, I wouldn’t go back to it as much. I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’ll eat this.” It’s more automatic than that. I notice the container, take a bite, then another.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know if it’s the feeling or the food. Maybe both, but I’d be guessing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is something you do to manage or cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. “Cope with emotions” sounds… more organized than it feels when I’m doing it. I’m not usually thinking, well, I’m sad or tense, so I’ll eat the leftovers.\n\nSometimes I suppose I might feel better for a few minutes, but then again sometimes I’m just standing at the fridge because the container is right there. My brother used to eat straight from the pot, which drove my father crazy—he’d say, “Use a plate, we’re not animals,” and now I hear that sometimes when I’m leaning over the sink.\n\nSo… maybe it’s connected to feelings, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it clearly. I wouldn’t want to give you an answer just because it sounds like the right explanation.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the act of eating leftovers is something that helps you regulate or manage your emotions in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it regulating, exactly. That sounds like something you do on purpose, like checking the thermostat or taking deep breaths or… I don’t know, those little meditation tapes my wife used to have with the ocean sounds. I always just ended up thinking about whether the tide was coming in or going out.\n\nWith the leftovers, I don’t really know what it’s doing emotionally. Sometimes I feel calmer while I’m eating, maybe, but I couldn’t say if that’s the point of it or just because eating is eating. It’s more like I notice the food and then I’m eating it before I’ve really thought through why.\n\nSo… maybe in some way, but I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers if you were in a very good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would. I’m not sure, honestly. Away from home… it might be different, because the leftovers wouldn’t be sitting there in the same way, in my own fridge, in the same containers and all that.

But then again, I’ve eaten things just because they were there before. Hotels are funny for that—those little breakfasts where everything is in bins and you take more than you meant to. Although I suppose that’s not really leftovers, is it? My wife always used to take the tiny jams and then never use them. We had a drawer full of them once.

So, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to whether it happens the same way somewhere else. At home it’s just… easier to drift into it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is… at night, after dinner, I’ll start picking at leftovers in the kitchen, and I tell myself, “That’s enough, Alejandro,” but somehow my hand just keeps going back. It’s like the sensible part of me is there, talking, but it’s not really in charge.  \n\nAnd, you know, I never used to think much of leftovers—my mother hated wasting food, so that’s probably stuck with me—but this feels different. But no, to your question, I haven’t noticed anything sexual about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat the leftovers, like something bad would happen if you didn't?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it feels like something bad would happen, exactly. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty if you don't finish the leftovers?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. My mother could stretch leftovers for days—she had this way of making it seem almost wrong to throw anything out.\n\nBut if I don’t finish them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed whether I feel guilty after. Sometimes I just put the lid back on and tell myself I’ll deal with it tomorrow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I tell myself I’m just having a bite, just tidying up the container a little, but then… once I start picking at it, it’s like the sensible part of me is saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, close the fridge.\n\nAnd my hand still goes back in anyway. It reminds me of when my uncle used to stand in front of the icebox after dinner—he’d say he was “checking the inventory,” which was ridiculous. But yes, with me, late at night especially, I don’t feel quite in control once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you feel better when you're feeling down or bored in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. In the evenings, I do get sort of restless, and the kitchen is just… there. I mean, I do want it in the moment, I suppose, or I wouldn’t open the container. But what brings me in is more that once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it stops feeling like a choice.\n\nIt’s like the sensible part of me is saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, close it up,” and then my hand keeps going back for more anyway. My mother used to save every scrap in little bowls with foil over them, and you’d have to sort of excavate the fridge to find anything… but with me, it’s not really about enjoying the leftovers. It’s that once I’ve started, I have a hard time stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you deal with any uncomfortable feelings or emotions you might be having in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it helps me deal with feelings, exactly. I don’t always know what I’m feeling in the evening, to be honest. Sometimes it’s just quiet in the house, and then I notice the fridge, and then one thing leads to another.\n\nI had a neighbor years ago who used to walk every night after dinner, rain or shine, and I used to think, well, that’s a good habit, isn’t it? Me, I tend to drift around the kitchen instead. But whether I’m trying to push down sadness or boredom or anything like that… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It may be more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to calm down or relax when you're feeling restless in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, like calming down or relaxing. Maybe there’s some of that, but I don’t really notice myself thinking, “I need to settle down, let me eat.” It’s more like I’m in the kitchen, a little aimless, and then I see the container.\n\nRestless, yes, evenings can feel sort of… loose, you know? After dinner there isn’t always much structure. When I was working, at least you had the next morning sort of pressing on you. Now the hours stretch out. My brother-in-law used to do crossword puzzles every night with a cup of tea, very orderly man. I never got into that.\n\nSo I don’t know if the leftovers relax me. They might just give me something to do for a few minutes, and then it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you weren't able to eat the leftovers when you want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant distress. I mean, if the leftovers weren’t there, I suppose I might feel annoyed or sort of unsettled for a bit, but I’m not sure. Sometimes I don’t even know I want them until I’m already standing there with the fridge open.\n\nIt’s funny, my aunt had this old refrigerator that made such a racket you could hear it from the living room, and every time it kicked on everyone would joke the house was about to take off. Mine is quieter, so maybe I notice the light more than the sound—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really tested it, I guess. I don’t know that I’d be deeply upset if I couldn’t eat them. I might just grumble and look around for something else to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're compelled to eat the leftovers, even if you're not hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think compelled might be the word, though I don’t love admitting that. It’s not always that I’m hungry. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, there’s this part of me going, “That’s enough, Alejandro, leave it alone,” but my hand still goes back in the container.\n\nIt reminds me of when my mother used to say you had to put food away properly or everyone would keep “evening it out,” taking little bites until it was gone. I used to laugh at that, but now I sort of do it myself. I’ll tell myself I’m just having one more forkful, and then somehow I’m back again. So yes, there is a feeling of not quite steering it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers even if you weren't thinking about your mother or your childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that my mother or childhood is always in my head when it happens. Sometimes those memories come up after I’ve already noticed what I’m doing, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing there with the leftovers, and then I think of something she used to say, but I don’t know if that’s the reason I’m eating them. It might just be… the food is there. The kitchen is quiet. I’m up later than I meant to be.\n\nMy mother had all these little sayings about food, but then again everyone’s mother did, probably. So I don’t want to make too much of it. I guess I haven’t really separated it out enough to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers helps you avoid feeling empty or bored when you're alone in the evenings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, evenings can be quiet, sure, but I haven’t really thought of it as “empty” exactly. Bored maybe, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’m eating the leftovers to avoid that feeling.\n\nI’ll wander into the kitchen and then I’m there, you know. She’s very firm about that.\n\nBut then… maybe if I were feeling different, I wouldn’t go back to it as much. I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’ll eat this.” It’s more automatic than that. I notice the container, take a bite, then another.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know if it’s the feeling or the food. Maybe both, but I’d be guessing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is something you do to manage or cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. “Cope with emotions” sounds… more organized than it feels when I’m doing it. I’m not usually thinking, well, I’m sad or tense, so I’ll eat the leftovers.\n\nSometimes I suppose I might feel better for a few minutes, but then again sometimes I’m just standing at the fridge because the container is right there. My brother used to eat straight from the pot, which drove my father crazy—he’d say, “Use a plate, we’re not animals,” and now I hear that sometimes when I’m leaning over the sink.\n\nSo… maybe it’s connected to feelings, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it clearly. I wouldn’t want to give you an answer just because it sounds like the right explanation.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the act of eating leftovers is something that helps you regulate or manage your emotions in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it regulating, exactly. That sounds like something you do on purpose, like checking the thermostat or taking deep breaths or… I don’t know, those little meditation tapes my wife used to have with the ocean sounds. I always just ended up thinking about whether the tide was coming in or going out.\n\nWith the leftovers, I don’t really know what it’s doing emotionally. Sometimes I feel calmer while I’m eating, maybe, but I couldn’t say if that’s the point of it or just because eating is eating. It’s more like I notice the food and then I’m eating it before I’ve really thought through why.\n\nSo… maybe in some way, but I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers if you were in a very good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would. I mean, if I’ve already had dinner with everyone—proper dinner, not just picking at something—I’m usually not hungry later. But I still end up opening the fridge and, you know, lifting the lid on one container, tasting a bit of this, then maybe something else.\n\nIt’s funny, my mother used to save everything in those margarine tubs, so you never knew if you were getting butter or gravy. Maybe that’s why I still have to look in all the containers. But yes… even in a good mood, I think I might still do it. It doesn’t always seem to start from feeling bad. Sometimes I’m just not hungry, but I’m eating the leftovers anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat the leftovers if you were in a different environment, away from your home and its familiar routines?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Away from home… it might be different, because the leftovers wouldn’t be sitting there in the same way, in my own fridge, in the same containers and all that.\n\nBut then again, I’ve eaten things just because they were there before. Hotels are funny for that—those little breakfasts where everything is in bins and you take more than you meant to. Although I suppose that’s not really leftovers, is it? My wife always used to take the tiny jams and then never use them. We had a drawer full of them once.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to whether it happens the same way somewhere else. At home it’s just… easier to drift into it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
